Safety components and mitigations</span></span></a></div></li><li class="Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__item___OceIg Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__item--level-2___AEntj Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__item--leaf___SN9hT" data-testid="NavList-item"><div class="Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__itemContent___TyAP4"><a class="Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__link___lJiXk" data-testid="NavList-link" href="#10-best-practices-for-deploying-and-adopting-github-copilot-agentic-features"><span class="Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__labelArea___N5Ya6"><span class="Primer_Brand__NavList-module__NavList__label___cZ4Jt">10. Learn more about GitHub Copilot agentic features</span></span></a></div></li></ul></nav></div></div><div data-container="article" style="grid-area:content" data-search="article-body"><div id="article-contents" data-article-body="true"><div class="MarkdownContent_markdownBody__v5MYy markdown-body"><h2 id="what-is-an-application-card" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#what-is-an-application-card">What is an Application Card?<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>GitHub’s application and platform cards are intended to help you understand how our AI technology works, the choices application owners can make that influence application performance and behavior, and the importance of considering the whole application, including the technology, the people, and the environment. Application cards are created for AI applications and platform cards are created for AI platform services. These resources can support the development or deployment of your own applications and can be shared with users or stakeholders impacted by them.</p>
<p>As part of its commitment to responsible AI, GitHub adheres to Microsoft&#x27;s <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/principles-and-approach/?msockid=3da790040c776d6f2b5485e40de56c06#ai-principles">six core principles</a>: fairness, reliability and safety, privacy and security, inclusiveness, transparency, and accountability. These principles are embedded in the <a href="https://cdn-dynmedia-1.microsoft.com/is/content/microsoftcorp/microsoft/final/en-us/microsoft-brand/documents/Microsoft-Responsible-AI-Standard-General-Requirements.pdf?culture=en-us&amp;country=us">Responsible AI Standard</a>, which guides teams in designing, building, and testing AI applications. Application and Platform Cards play a key role in operationalizing these principles by offering transparency around capabilities, intended uses, and limitations. For further insight, readers are encouraged to explore Microsoft’s <a href="https://cdn-dynmedia-1.microsoft.com/is/content/microsoftcorp/microsoft/msc/documents/presentations/CSR/Responsible-AI-Transparency-Report-2025-vertical.pdf">Responsible AI Transparency Report</a> and <a href="/en/site-policy/github-terms">GitHub Terms</a>.</p>
<h2 id="1-overview" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#1-overview">1. Overview<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>GitHub Copilot includes several agentic features that go beyond suggestion and conversation—they can review code, take action on your behalf, and build applications. This card covers the following experiences:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Copilot code review</strong>: Reviews pull request diffs and metadata on GitHub.com, producing feedback comments and suggested changes.</li>
<li><strong>Copilot cloud agent</strong>: An asynchronous agent on GitHub.com that can create branches, write code, and open pull requests in response to assigned issues. The cloud agent runs in an ephemeral, firewalled environment with automated security scanning.</li>
<li><strong>Copilot CLI</strong>: A command-line tool that can create and modify files, execute commands, and perform multi-step tasks. By default, filesystem access is scoped to the directory where the CLI was started. Permission prompts depend on the active permission mode. When computer use is enabled, Copilot CLI can also interact with desktop applications outside that directory scope.</li>
<li><strong>Copilot SDK</strong>: A programmatic library that allows developers to build custom AI-powered applications using Copilot. The SDK communicates with Copilot CLI over JSON-RPC and supports custom agents, MCP server integrations, lifecycle hooks, and session management.</li>
<li><strong>GitHub Copilot app</strong>: A desktop application for directing agent sessions across local repositories, git worktrees, and cloud sandboxes. The app uses GitHub Copilot CLI and GitHub Copilot SDK as its foundation and adds a user interface for parallel sessions, quick chats, GitHub issue and pull request workflows, automations, and canvases. When computer use is enabled, the app can also interact with desktop applications.</li>
</ul>
<p>These features share common principles—human oversight, review of outputs, and responsible use—but differ in their execution environments, permissions, and data flows. The sections below describe each in context.</p>
<h2 id="2-key-terms" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#2-key-terms">2. Key terms<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>The following list provides a glossary of key terms related to GitHub Copilot Agents:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Code suggestion</strong>: A specific code change proposed by Copilot code review as part of its feedback on a pull request. Code suggestions are presented as suggested changes that can be applied with a couple of clicks.</li>
<li><strong>Content filtering</strong>: A safety system that scans prompts and responses to detect and block harmful, offensive, or insecure content before it is shown to the user.</li>
<li><strong>Computer use</strong>: An optional capability in GitHub Copilot CLI and GitHub Copilot app that allows an agent to interact with desktop applications. Its tools can read accessible application content and visual context through the operating system&#x27;s accessibility tree or screenshots when visual context is needed, click controls, enter and edit text, press keys, scroll, drag, and navigate workflows across applications.</li>
<li><strong>Custom instructions</strong>: Natural language descriptions of coding style and best practices that a repository maintainer can configure to guide Copilot code review&#x27;s feedback. Custom instructions help Copilot understand the conventions and standards of a specific codebase.</li>
<li><strong>Hallucination</strong>: A phenomenon where a language model generates output that sounds plausible but is factually incorrect, unsupported by the provided context, or entirely fabricated. In code review, hallucination can manifest as feedback that highlights problems that do not exist or are based on misunderstandings of the code.</li>
<li><strong>Large language model (LLM)</strong>: A type of neural network trained on a large body of text data that can generate, analyze, and transform natural language and code. Copilot Agents use one or more LLMs to process context and produce responses.</li>
<li><strong>Ephemeral development environment</strong>: A temporary, isolated compute environment created for each cloud agent session. The environment is destroyed after the session ends, ensuring that no state persists between runs.</li>
<li><strong>Firewall</strong>: A network-level control enabled by default for the cloud agent that prevents outbound connections to unauthorized hosts, protecting against accidental or malicious exfiltration of code or data.</li>
<li><strong>AI credit</strong>: A unit of consumption for Copilot features. Each use of Copilot code review consumes AI credits.</li>
<li><strong>Permission prompt</strong>: An interactive confirmation step in Copilot CLI that asks the user to approve an action—such as modifying a file, executing a command, or accessing files outside the current directory—before the agent proceeds. Permission prompts are a key safety mechanism for local agentic execution.</li>
<li><strong>Copilot SDK</strong>: A programmatic library (<code>@github/copilot-sdk</code>) that lets developers build custom AI-powered applications by creating sessions, sending prompts, and receiving streaming responses from Copilot. The SDK communicates with Copilot CLI over JSON-RPC.</li>
<li><strong>JSON-RPC</strong>: The communication protocol used between the Copilot SDK and Copilot CLI. The SDK sends structured requests to the CLI process, which handles model interaction and tool execution.</li>
<li><strong>Custom agent (SDK)</strong>: A named agent configuration within the SDK that has its own system prompt, scoped tools, and optional MCP servers. The SDK runtime can automatically delegate to sub-agents based on user intent.</li>
<li><strong>Hooks (SDK)</strong>: Lifecycle callbacks in the Copilot SDK that let developers inject custom logic at specific points during a session, such as before or after tool use, on session start or end, and on error.</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="3-key-features-or-capabilities" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#3-key-features-or-capabilities">3. Key features or capabilities<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>The key features and capabilities outlined here describe what GitHub Copilot Agents are designed to do and how they perform across supported tasks.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Automated code review feedback</strong>: When a user requests a review from Copilot, it scans the code changes plus additional relevant context and provides feedback on the code. Feedback may include natural language comments and specific code suggestions linked to particular lines and files.</li>
<li><strong>Customizable review guidance</strong>: Copilot code review can be customized with custom instructions—natural language descriptions of coding style and best practices—so that feedback reflects a repository&#x27;s conventions and standards.</li>
<li><strong>Autonomous pull request creation</strong>: The Copilot cloud agent can pick up a task from an issue, a pull request comment, or Copilot Chat, create a branch, generate tailored code changes, and open a pull request. After the initial pull request is created, the agent can iterate based on your feedback and reviews.</li>
<li><strong>Ephemeral, firewalled execution</strong>: While working on a task, the cloud agent has access to its own ephemeral development environment where it can make changes to code, execute automated tests, and run linters. A firewall is enabled by default to prevent data exfiltration.</li>
<li><strong>Automated security scanning</strong>: During code generation, the cloud agent automatically analyzes newly generated code for security vulnerabilities using CodeQL, secret scanning, and dependency analysis, and attempts to resolve any issues before they are introduced.</li>
<li><strong>External integrations</strong>: The cloud agent can receive information and context from MCP like workIQ and Microsoft 365, and external applications like Microsoft Teams, Linear, Slack, and Jira, enabling teams to assign tasks and track progress directly within their existing workflows.</li>
<li><strong>Local agentic execution (Copilot CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI provides a chat-like interface in the terminal that can autonomously create and modify files, execute commands, and perform multi-step tasks. By default, filesystem access is scoped to the directory where the CLI was started. The active permission mode determines whether the CLI prompts before modifying files or running commands.</li>
<li><strong>Desktop application interaction (computer use)</strong>: In local Copilot CLI and Copilot app sessions, computer use can read accessible application content and visual context through the operating system&#x27;s accessibility tree or screenshots when visual context is needed, click controls, enter and edit text, press keys, scroll, drag, and navigate workflows across applications.</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="4-intended-uses" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#4-intended-uses">4. Intended uses<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>GitHub Copilot Agents can be used in multiple scenarios across a variety of industries. Some examples of use cases include:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Supplementing human code review</strong>: Copilot code review is intended to quickly provide feedback on a developer&#x27;s code, enabling developers to get code ready to merge more quickly and increasing overall code quality.</li>
<li><strong>Codebase maintenance</strong>: The cloud agent can tackle security-related fixes, dependency upgrades, and targeted refactoring.</li>
<li><strong>Feature development</strong>: The cloud agent can implement incremental feature requests, develop additional test suites, and create or update documentation.</li>
<li><strong>Prototyping new projects</strong>: The cloud agent and Copilot CLI can greenfield new concepts, helping developers explore ideas quickly.</li>
<li><strong>Setting up your environment (CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI can run commands in your terminal to set up your local environment to work on existing projects.</li>
<li><strong>Finding the right command (CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI can suggest commands to perform tasks you&#x27;re trying to complete, and explain unfamiliar commands in natural language.</li>
<li><strong>Working with legacy and GUI-only applications (computer use)</strong>: Computer use can help with workflows in local desktop applications that do not provide an API, command-line interface, or MCP integration. Users should describe the outcome, applications, and important constraints, then review the actions and result.</li>
<li><strong>Building custom AI applications (SDK)</strong>: The Copilot SDK enables developers to build applications that leverage Copilot for code generation, natural language interaction, and task automation in their own products and workflows.</li>
<li><strong>Multi-agent orchestration (SDK)</strong>: Using custom agents and sub-agents, developers can build sophisticated workflows where multiple specialized agents collaborate on complex tasks, with automatic delegation based on user intent.</li>
<li><strong>Extending applications with external tools (SDK)</strong>: The SDK&#x27;s MCP server support allows developers to connect their applications to external data sources and services, expanding the range of tasks their agents can perform.</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="5-models-and-training-data" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#5-models-and-training-data">5. Models and training data<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>GitHub Copilot Agents leverage a variety of AI models to power the experience that users see. For a comparison of the models available for Copilot, see <a href="/en/copilot/reference/ai-models/model-comparison">AI model comparison</a>. For the full list of supported models, see <a href="/en/copilot/reference/ai-models/supported-models">Supported AI models in GitHub Copilot</a>. For information on where models are hosted, see <a href="/en/copilot/reference/ai-models/model-hosting">Hosting of models for GitHub Copilot</a>. To learn more about the data used to train the foundation models behind GitHub Copilot Agents, refer to the linked AI model comparison above and <a href="https://github.com/features/copilot#faq">What data has GitHub Copilot been trained on?</a> in the GitHub Copilot FAQ.</p>
<p>Copilot code review is a purpose-built product that uses a carefully tuned mix of models, prompts, and system behaviors to deliver consistent, high-quality feedback across a wide range of codebases. Model switching is not supported, as changing the model is likely to compromise reliability, user experience, and the quality of review comments. Copilot code review may use models that are not enabled on your organization&#x27;s &quot;Models&quot; settings page.</p>
<p>The Copilot cloud agent uses a large language model to reason about tasks, generate code, and leverage tools within its ephemeral development environment. The agent has been evaluated across a variety of programming languages. English is the primary supported language for prompts and responses.</p>
<p>Copilot CLI uses a large language model to reason about tasks, generate code, modify files, and execute commands in your local terminal environment. The agent has been evaluated across a variety of programming languages. English is the primary supported language for prompts and responses.</p>
<p>The Copilot SDK communicates with Copilot CLI over JSON-RPC, using the same underlying models and capabilities. Applications built with the SDK use the same models available to the authenticated Copilot user or organization. Developers can also bring their own API keys (BYOK) to use custom model providers.</p>
<h2 id="6-performance" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#6-performance">6. Performance<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<h3 id="differences-by-experience" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#differences-by-experience">Differences by experience<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<h4 id="copilot-cloud-agent" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-cloud-agent">Copilot cloud agent<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>The Copilot cloud agent works by using a combination of natural language processing and machine learning to understand your task and make changes in a codebase. This process can be broken down into a number of steps:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Prompt processing</strong>: The task provided through an issue, pull request comment, or Copilot Chat message is combined with other relevant, contextual information to form a prompt. That prompt is sent to a large language model for processing. Inputs can take the form of plain natural language, code snippets, or images.</li>
<li><strong>Language model analysis</strong>: The prompt is passed through a large language model, which is a neural network that has been trained on a large body of data. The language model analyzes the input prompt to help the agent reason about the task and leverage necessary tools.</li>
<li><strong>Response generation</strong>: The language model generates a response based on its analysis of the prompt. This response can take the form of natural language suggestions and code suggestions.</li>
<li><strong>Output formatting</strong>: Once the agent completes its first run, it updates the pull request description with the changes it made. The agent may include supplemental information about resources it could not access and provide suggestions on steps to resolve. You may provide feedback by commenting within the pull request or explicitly mentioning the agent (<code>@copilot</code>). The agent will then resubmit that feedback for further analysis and respond with updated changes.</li>
</ol>
<p>Copilot cloud agent is intended to provide the most relevant solution for task resolution. However, it may not always provide the answer you are looking for. You are responsible for reviewing and validating responses generated by Copilot cloud agent to ensure they are accurate and appropriate.</p>
<h4 id="copilot-code-review" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-code-review">Copilot code review<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>Copilot code review inspects your code and provides feedback using a combination of natural language processing and machine learning. This process can be broken down into a number of steps:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Input processing</strong>: The code changes are combined with other relevant, contextual information (for example, the pull request&#x27;s title and body), and any custom instructions that have been defined, to form a prompt. That prompt is sent to a large language model.</li>
<li><strong>Language model analysis</strong>: The prompt is passed through the Copilot language model, which is a neural network that has been trained on a large body of text data. The language model analyzes the input prompt.</li>
<li><strong>Response generation</strong>: The language model generates a response based on its analysis of the input prompt. This response can take the form of natural language suggestions and code suggestions.</li>
<li><strong>Output formatting</strong>: The response is presented to the user either directly in a supported editor or as a pull request review on GitHub.com, providing code feedback linked to specific lines of specific files. Where Copilot has provided a code suggestion, the suggestion is presented as a suggested change, which can be applied with a couple of clicks.</li>
</ol>
<h4 id="copilot-cli" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-cli">Copilot CLI<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>Copilot CLI works by using a combination of natural language processing and machine learning to understand your task and make changes in a codebase. This process can be broken down into a number of steps:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Input processing</strong>: Your input is combined with relevant contextual information to form a prompt. That prompt is sent to a large language model for processing. Inputs can take the form of plain natural language, code snippets, or references to files in your terminal.</li>
<li><strong>Language model analysis</strong>: The prompt is passed through a large language model, which is a neural network that has been trained on a large body of data. The language model analyzes the input prompt to help the agent reason about the task and use the necessary tools.</li>
<li><strong>Response generation</strong>: The language model generates a response based on its analysis of the prompt. This response can take the form of natural language suggestions, code suggestions, file modifications, and command executions.</li>
<li><strong>Output formatting</strong>: The response is formatted and presented using syntax highlighting, indentation, and other formatting features. The agent may also execute commands in your local environment and create, edit, or delete files in your file system in order to complete the task. All such actions require explicit permission prompts.</li>
</ol>
<p>You may provide feedback to the agent after it returns a response in the interactive chat window. The agent will resubmit that feedback to the language model for further analysis and return an additional response.</p>
<h4 id="copilot-sdk" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-sdk">Copilot SDK<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>The Copilot SDK provides a programmatic interface to Copilot&#x27;s agentic capabilities. Applications built with the SDK follow this process:</p>
<ol>
<li><strong>Session creation</strong>: The application creates a session with the SDK, specifying the model, system prompt, available tools, custom agents, MCP servers, and hooks. The SDK establishes a JSON-RPC connection to Copilot CLI.</li>
<li><strong>Prompt submission</strong>: User input is submitted to the session. The SDK routes the prompt (along with session context) to the CLI, which forwards it to the language model.</li>
<li><strong>Agent execution</strong>: The language model reasons about the task and may invoke tools, delegate to sub-agents, or connect to MCP servers. Lifecycle hooks fire at each stage, allowing the application to inject custom logic.</li>
<li><strong>Response streaming</strong>: Responses are streamed back to the application, which can present them in any format appropriate for its interface. The SDK provides structured events for text, tool calls, errors, and completion signals.</li>
</ol>
<h2 id="7-limitations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#7-limitations">7. Limitations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>Understanding GitHub Copilot agentic features&#x27; limitations is crucial to determine they are used within safe and effective boundaries. While we encourage customers to leverage these features in their innovative solutions or applications, it&#x27;s important to note that they were not designed for every possible scenario. We encourage users to refer to <a href="/en/site-policy/github-terms">GitHub Terms</a> as well as the following considerations when choosing a use case:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Missed code quality problems</strong>: Copilot may not identify all of the problems that are present in code, especially where changes are large or complex. To ensure that all relevant problems are identified and corrected, Copilot code review should be supplemented with careful human code review.</li>
<li><strong>False positives</strong>: Copilot code review has a risk of hallucination—it may highlight problems in reviewed code that do not exist or are based on misunderstandings of the code. Comments generated by Copilot code review should be carefully reviewed and considered before taking action and making changes.</li>
<li><strong>Inaccurate or insecure code suggestions</strong>: As part of its comments, Copilot code review may provide specific code suggestions. The code generated may appear to be valid but may not actually be semantically or syntactically correct, or may not correctly resolve the problem identified in the comment. In addition, code generated by Copilot may contain security vulnerabilities or other issues. You should always carefully review and test code generated by Copilot.</li>
<li><strong>Potential biases</strong>: Copilot&#x27;s training data is drawn from existing code repositories, which may contain biases and errors that can be perpetuated by the tool. Additionally, Copilot code review may be biased toward certain programming languages or coding styles, which can lead to suboptimal or incomplete feedback.</li>
<li><strong>Limited scope (cloud agent)</strong>: The language model used by the cloud agent has been trained on a large body of code but still has a limited scope and may not be able to handle certain code structures or obscure programming languages. For each language, the quality of suggestions depends on the volume and diversity of training data for that language.</li>
<li><strong>Inaccurate code (cloud agent)</strong>: The cloud agent may generate code that appears to be valid but may not actually be semantically or syntactically correct, or may not accurately reflect the intent of the developer. You should carefully review and test generated code, particularly when dealing with critical or sensitive applications.</li>
<li><strong>Security risks (cloud agent)</strong>: The cloud agent generates code and natural language based on the context of an issue or comment within a repository, which can potentially expose sensitive information or vulnerabilities if not used carefully. You should review all outputs generated by the agent thoroughly prior to merging.</li>
<li><strong>Public code matches (cloud agent)</strong>: The cloud agent may generate code that is a match or near match of publicly available code, even if the &quot;Suggestions matching public code&quot; policy is set to &quot;Block.&quot; If this happens, Copilot will show matches in the agent session logs with a link to display details of the matched code.</li>
<li><strong>Limited scope (CLI)</strong>: The language model used by Copilot CLI has been trained on a large body of code but still has a limited scope and may not be able to handle certain code structures or obscure programming languages. For each language, the quality of suggestions depends on the volume and diversity of training data for that language.</li>
<li><strong>Inaccurate code (CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI may generate code that appears to be valid but may not actually be semantically or syntactically correct, or may not accurately reflect the intent of the developer. You should carefully review and test generated code, particularly when dealing with critical or sensitive applications.</li>
<li><strong>Security risks (CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI generates code and natural language based on the context of your local environment, which can potentially expose sensitive information or vulnerabilities if not used carefully. You should review all outputs generated by the agent thoroughly.</li>
<li><strong>Public code matches (CLI)</strong>: Copilot CLI may generate code that is a match or near match of publicly available code, even if the &quot;Suggestions matching public code&quot; policy is set to &quot;Block.&quot;</li>
<li><strong>Command execution risks (CLI)</strong>: Additional caution is required when asking or allowing Copilot CLI to execute a command, particularly regarding the potential destructiveness of some suggested commands. You may encounter commands for file deletion or hard drive formatting, which can cause problems if used incorrectly. You are ultimately responsible for the commands executed by Copilot CLI.</li>
<li><strong>Interface interpretation (computer use)</strong>: Computer use may select the wrong control, enter text in the wrong location, or have difficulty with non-standard or dynamic controls and complex workflows. Changes in timing or window state can produce different results, cause computer use to repeat an action, or prevent it from continuing.</li>
<li><strong>Unintended actions (computer use)</strong>: Ambiguous instructions or unexpected on-screen content can cause computer use to take unintended actions. These actions may modify or expose data or affect connected accounts. Users should keep tasks narrow, specify important constraints, and monitor the active application.</li>
<li><strong>On-screen information (computer use)</strong>: Application windows may display sensitive information or information about other people. Users should only enable computer use for applications and tasks whose visible content is appropriate to provide as context.</li>
<li><strong>Local and platform constraints (computer use)</strong>: Computer use requires a local session and is available on macOS and Windows. On macOS, Accessibility and Screen Recording permissions are required. If the bundled plugin, helper, or MCP server is unavailable or disconnected, computer-use actions cannot run.</li>
<li><strong>Always allowed applications (computer use)</strong>: An application that a user chooses to always allow can be controlled in later sessions without another application-level prompt. <strong>Always allow</strong> is not appropriate for every application, particularly applications that contain sensitive information or support high-impact actions.</li>
<li><strong>Inherited limitations (SDK)</strong>: Because the Copilot SDK communicates with Copilot CLI, applications built with the SDK inherit the same model limitations, including limited scope for certain programming languages and the potential for inaccurate or insecure code generation.</li>
<li><strong>Custom agent complexity (SDK)</strong>: Incorrectly configured custom agents, tools, or hooks may produce unexpected behavior. Developers are responsible for testing and validating the behavior of their custom agent configurations.</li>
<li><strong>MCP server trust (SDK)</strong>: MCP servers connected through the SDK can expose tools and data from external sources. Developers must ensure that connected MCP servers are trustworthy, as malicious or misconfigured servers could introduce harmful behavior or expose sensitive data.</li>
<li><strong>BYOK model variance (SDK)</strong>: When using bring-your-own-key configurations with third-party model providers, behavior may differ from GitHub-hosted models. Developers are responsible for evaluating the safety and quality of responses from their chosen provider.</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="8-evaluations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#8-evaluations">8. Evaluations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>Performance and safety evaluations assess whether AI applications are operating reliably and securely by examining factors like groundedness, relevance, and coherence while identifying the risks of generating harmful content. The following evaluations were conducted with safety components already in place, which are also described in <a href="#9-safety-components-and-mitigations">9. Safety components and mitigations</a>.</p>
<h3 id="performance-and-quality-evaluations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#performance-and-quality-evaluations">Performance and quality evaluations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>GitHub Copilot Agents are evaluated across its supported surfaces using a combination of industry-standard benchmarks (e.g., SWE-Bench) and internally developed evaluation suites. Benchmark tasks are sourced from public open-source repositories and synthetic scenarios; no real user queries or customer code are used. Each evaluation includes multiple independent runs to account for nondeterminism in model outputs. Key metrics include resolution rate (percentage of tasks successfully completed), token efficiency, latency, and tool call reliability. Models are re-evaluated when updates are made and monitored continuously in production via error rates, response latency, and aggregate usage patterns.</p>
<h3 id="performance-and-quality-evaluation-methods" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#performance-and-quality-evaluation-methods">Performance and quality evaluation methods<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>New models for GitHub Copilot Agents undergo a staged evaluation process before deployment. Integrator teams run benchmark suites specific to their surface, testing the model on representative coding tasks such as bug fixes, code generation, and multi-file refactoring. Results are reviewed against established baselines and existing production models. Models must meet or exceed baseline performance across key metrics like resolution rate, token efficiency, and latency, before advancing to the next stage. A cross-functional review board makes a formal go/no-go decision before any model is approved for user-facing deployment.</p>
<h3 id="risk-and-safety-evaluations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#risk-and-safety-evaluations">Risk and safety evaluations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>Evaluating potential risks associated with AI-generated content is essential for safeguarding against content risks with varying degrees of severity. This includes evaluating an AI application&#x27;s predisposition towards generating harmful content or testing vulnerabilities to jailbreak attacks. For GitHub, we conduct performance evaluations, including those which are adapted for coding purposes from <a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-foundry/concepts/evaluation-evaluators/risk-safety-evaluators">Microsoft Foundry</a>:</p>
<ul>
<li>Hate and unfairness</li>
<li>Sexual</li>
<li>Violence</li>
<li>Self-harm</li>
<li>Protected material</li>
<li>Jailbreak</li>
<li>Code vulnerability</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="evaluation-data-for-quality-and-safety" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#evaluation-data-for-quality-and-safety">Evaluation data for quality and safety<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>Our evaluation data is custom-built to assess AI application performance across key areas of <strong>safety</strong> and <strong>quality</strong>, simulating real-world scenarios and risks. We begin by identifying relevant evaluation aspects of concern based on multi-disciplinary research and expert input. These concerns are translated into targeted evaluation objectives and guide formulation of evaluation metrics. For <strong>safety</strong>, we create adversarial prompts to elicit undesirable or edge-case responses, which are then scored using AI-assisted annotators trained to assess alignment with GitHub’s standards. For <strong>quality</strong>, we craft rubric-based prompts relevant to scenarios including evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) applications and agents. Datasets are curated from diverse sources including synthetic and public datasets to simulate real-world user scenarios. Using the curated datasets, both evaluations undergo iterative refinement and human alignment to improve metric efficacy and reliability. This methodology forms the foundation of repeatable, rigorous assessments that reflect how customers use evaluations to build better AI.</p>
<h3 id="custom-evaluations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#custom-evaluations">Custom evaluations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>Copilot agentic features have been subject to RAI red teaming to identify and address potential risks. We continue to monitor the efficacy and safety of these features over time. For more information, see <a href="https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2023/08/07/microsoft-ai-red-team-building-future-of-safer-ai/">Microsoft AI Red Team building future of safer AI</a> on the Microsoft security blog.</p>
<h2 id="9-safety-components-and-mitigations" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#9-safety-components-and-mitigations">9. Safety components and mitigations<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<h3 id="copilot-cloud-agent-1" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-cloud-agent-1">Copilot cloud agent<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Privilege escalation controls</strong>: The cloud agent only responds to interactions from users with repository write access. Actions workflows triggered by pull requests raised by the agent require approval from a user with write access before they will run. The agent filters hidden characters that might allow users to hide harmful instructions in comments or issue contents.</li>
<li><strong>Constrained permissions</strong>: The cloud agent only has access to the repository where it is creating a pull request and cannot access other repositories. It can only push to a single branch: the existing pull request branch when triggered via <code>@copilot</code>, or otherwise to a new <code>copilot/</code> branch. This means that Copilot cannot push directly to your default branch (for example, <code>main</code>). The agent does not have access to Actions organization or repository secrets—only secrets and variables specifically added to the <code>copilot</code> environment are passed to the agent.</li>
<li><strong>Ensuring traceability</strong>: The cloud agent&#x27;s commits are authored by Copilot, with the human who started the task marked as the co-author. This makes it easier to identify code generated by the agent and who initiated the task. The cloud agent&#x27;s commits are signed, so they appear as &quot;Verified&quot; on GitHub. This provides confidence that the commits were made by GitHub Copilot cloud agent and have not been altered. Each commit message includes a link to the agent session logs. This gives you a permanent link from any agent-authored commit to the full session logs, so you can understand why Copilot made a change during code review or trace it later for auditing purposes.</li>
<li><strong>Firewall for data exfiltration prevention</strong>: By default, the cloud agent has a firewall enabled to prevent exfiltration of code or other sensitive data, either accidentally or due to malicious user input.</li>
<li><strong>Automated security vulnerability detection</strong>: During code generation, the cloud agent automatically analyzes newly generated code for security vulnerabilities and attempts to resolve them. Analysis is performed using CodeQL (to identify potential vulnerabilities and errors), secret scanning (to ensure secrets aren&#x27;t introduced), and dependency analysis (to check for known vulnerabilities in referenced dependencies).</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="copilot-cli-1" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-cli-1">Copilot CLI<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Directory-scoped access</strong>: By default, Copilot CLI only has access to files and folders in, and below, the directory from which it was invoked. If the agent wishes to access files outside the current directory, it will ask for permission.</li>
<li><strong>Permission prompts for file modifications</strong>: Copilot CLI asks for permission before modifying files. You should ensure it is modifying the correct files before granting permission.</li>
<li><strong>Permission prompts for command execution</strong>: Copilot CLI asks for permission before executing commands that may be dangerous. You should review these commands carefully before giving permission to run.</li>
<li><strong>Configurable permissions</strong>: You can grant Copilot CLI specific permissions, or all permissions, by using the various command line options: for example, <code>--allow-tool=[TOOLS...]</code>, <code>--allow-all-tools</code>, <code>--allow-all</code> (or its slash command equivalent <code>/allow-all</code> for use in an interactive session). For more information, see <a href="/en/copilot/reference/copilot-cli-reference/cli-command-reference#command-line-options">GitHub Copilot CLI command reference</a>. Typically, when you use Copilot CLI in autopilot mode, you will grant it full permissions to allow it to complete a task autonomously, without requiring you to approve activity as it works on the task. For more information, see <a href="/en/copilot/concepts/agents/copilot-cli/autopilot">Allowing GitHub Copilot CLI to work autonomously</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Security considerations</strong>: For more information about security practices while using Copilot CLI, see <a href="/en/copilot/concepts/copilot-surfaces/copilot-cli#security-considerations">GitHub Copilot CLI</a>.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="computer-use-in-github-copilot-cli-and-github-copilot-app" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#computer-use-in-github-copilot-cli-and-github-copilot-app">Computer use in GitHub Copilot CLI and GitHub Copilot app<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>Computer use is an optional local capability that allows an agent to interact with desktop applications on a user&#x27;s behalf. Like other agentic features, computer use has limitations. Because it interprets visual interfaces, changing interfaces or unexpected on-screen content can lead to inconsistent results, stalled workflows, or unintended actions. The following controls help users limit and interrupt its access.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Default-off activation</strong>: The bundled computer-use plugin is disabled by default. Users must enable it before its tools are available to an agent.</li>
<li><strong>Managed policy</strong>: Enterprise administrators can disable computer use through managed settings. Users cannot override the enterprise policy through local settings. For configuration details, see <a href="/en/copilot/reference/enterprise-administrators/enterprise-managed-settings#featurescomputeruse">Enterprise managed settings</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Tool permission controls</strong>: Computer-use application requests use the host permission system. Depending on the active permission mode, the host displays an approval prompt or approves the request automatically. Explicit deny rules take precedence, and a request fails closed if no permission handler accepts it.</li>
<li><strong>Operating system permissions</strong>: On macOS, computer use guides users through granting Accessibility permission to interact with controls and Screen Recording permission to inspect windows when visual context is needed.</li>
<li><strong>Application approval management</strong>: In GitHub Copilot app, users can review and remove saved application approvals. Removing an application deletes its saved approval for future sessions, but does not revoke access already granted in a running session. The host&#x27;s permission settings determine whether later requests display a prompt or are approved automatically.</li>
<li><strong>Session interruption</strong>: Users can interrupt an active local operation if computer use starts acting unexpectedly. In GitHub Copilot CLI, pressing <kbd>Esc</kbd> twice interrupts the current operation. In GitHub Copilot app, clicking <strong>Stop</strong> or pressing <kbd>Esc</kbd> interrupts it.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="copilot-sdk-1" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#copilot-sdk-1">Copilot SDK<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<ul>
<li><strong>Inherited CLI safety controls</strong>: The Copilot SDK communicates with Copilot CLI, inheriting its permission model and safety controls. Tool executions and file modifications still require appropriate permissions.</li>
<li><strong>Hook-based oversight</strong>: The SDK&#x27;s lifecycle hooks (such as <code>onPreToolUse</code> and <code>onPostToolUse</code>) allow developers to implement custom safety checks, audit logging, and approval workflows before and after tool execution.</li>
<li><strong>MCP server isolation</strong>: MCP servers run as separate processes. Developers can control which servers are available per session, limiting the scope of external tool access.</li>
<li><strong>Session scoping</strong>: Each SDK session is isolated with its own context, tools, and permissions. Developers can control what data and capabilities are available within each session.</li>
<li><strong>BYOK responsibility</strong>: When using bring-your-own-key configurations, prompts and responses are sent directly to the configured provider. Developers are responsible for reviewing the data handling policies of their chosen provider.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="github-copilot-app" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#github-copilot-app">GitHub Copilot app<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>The GitHub Copilot app is a desktop application for directing agent sessions across local repositories, git worktrees, and cloud sandboxes. The app uses GitHub Copilot CLI and GitHub Copilot SDK as its foundation and adds a user interface for parallel sessions, quick chats, GitHub issue and pull request workflows, automations, and canvases.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Workspace isolation</strong>: App sessions can run in separate git worktrees or cloud sandboxes, helping isolate concurrent tasks from each other and from the default branch.</li>
<li><strong>Inherited CLI and SDK safety controls</strong>: App sessions use GitHub Copilot CLI and GitHub Copilot SDK, so they inherit the same model limitations and safety controls for file access, file modification, command execution, session scoping, and connected tools.</li>
<li><strong>Human review before merge</strong>: The app surfaces generated changes, pull request status, CI results, review activity, and session history so users can inspect work before submitting reviews, enabling agent merge, or merging pull requests.</li>
<li><strong>Configurable integrations and automations</strong>: Users control which MCP servers, skills, canvas extensions, and automations are available in the app. These configurations should be reviewed before use because they can affect the tools, data, and background actions available to sessions.</li>
</ul>
<h3 id="data-handling-when-using-your-own-model-provider-cli" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#data-handling-when-using-your-own-model-provider-cli">Data handling when using your own model provider (CLI)<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h3>
<p>When you configure Copilot CLI to use your own model provider, your prompts, code context, and generated responses are sent directly to the provider you configure. They are not routed through GitHub. You are responsible for reviewing and complying with the terms of service and data handling policies of your chosen provider.</p>
<h4 id="telemetry" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#telemetry">Telemetry<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>When you use your own model provider without offline mode, Copilot CLI continues to send telemetry to GitHub as usual. This telemetry does not include your prompts or code, but it does include usage metadata.</p>
<p>If you enable offline mode by setting the <code>COPILOT_OFFLINE</code> environment variable to <code>true</code>, all telemetry is disabled. In offline mode, Copilot CLI only makes network requests to your configured model provider.</p>
<h4 id="authentication-and-feature-availability" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#authentication-and-feature-availability">Authentication and feature availability<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>GitHub authentication is not required when using your own model provider (BYOK). Without GitHub authentication, the following features are unavailable:</p>
<ul>
<li><code>/delegate</code>, which hands off the session to GitHub&#x27;s server-side Copilot</li>
<li>The GitHub MCP server</li>
<li>GitHub Code Search</li>
</ul>
<p>In offline mode, web-based tools such as <code>web_fetch</code> and GitHub Code Search are also disabled.</p>
<h4 id="no-fallback-to-github-hosted-models" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#no-fallback-to-github-hosted-models">No fallback to GitHub-hosted models<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h4>
<p>If your model provider configuration is invalid, Copilot CLI exits with an error. It does not fall back to GitHub-hosted models. Common failures, such as connection refused, authentication errors, model not found, and timeouts, produce user-friendly messages with actionable guidance.</p>
<h2 id="10-best-practices-for-deploying-and-adopting-github-copilot-agentic-features" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#10-best-practices-for-deploying-and-adopting-github-copilot-agentic-features">10. Best practices for deploying and adopting GitHub Copilot agentic features<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
<p>Responsible AI is a shared commitment between GitHub and its customers. While GitHub builds AI applications with safety, fairness, and transparency at the core, customers play a critical role in deploying and using these technologies responsibly within their own contexts. To support this partnership, we offer the following best practices for deployers and end users to help customers implement responsible AI effectively.</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Exercise caution and evaluate outcomes when using Copilot agentic features for consequential decisions or in sensitive domains</strong>: 
Consequential decisions are those that may have a legal or significant impact on a person&#x27;s access to education, employment, financial platforms, government benefits, healthcare, housing, insurance, legal platforms, or that could result in physical, psychological, or financial harm. Sensitive domains—such as financial platforms, healthcare, and housing—require particular care due to the potential for disproportionate impact on different groups of people. When using AI for decisions in these areas, make sure that impacted stakeholders can understand how decisions are made, appeal decisions, and update any relevant input data.</li>
<li><strong>Evaluate legal and regulatory considerations</strong>: 
Customers need to evaluate potential specific legal and regulatory obligations when using any AI platforms and solutions, which may not be appropriate for use in every industry or scenario. Additionally, AI platforms or solutions are not designed for and may not be used in ways prohibited in applicable terms of service and relevant codes of conduct.</li>
<li><strong>Exercise human oversight when appropriate</strong>: Human oversight is an important safeguard when interacting with AI applications. While we continuously improve our AI applications, AI might still make mistakes. The outputs generated may be inaccurate, incomplete, biased, misaligned, or irrelevant to your intended goals. This could happen due to various reasons, such as ambiguity in the inputs or limitations of the underlying models. As such, users should review the responses generated by Copilot agentic features and verify that they match their expectations and requirements.</li>
<li><strong>Be aware of the risk of overreliance</strong>: 
Overreliance on AI happens when users accept incorrect or incomplete AI outputs, mainly because mistakes in AI outputs may be hard to detect. For the end-user, overreliance could result in decreased productivity, loss of trust, application abandonment, financial loss, psychological harm, physical harm, among others. (e.g. a doctor accepts an incorrect AI output).</li>
<li><strong>Exercise caution when designing agentic AI in sensitive domains</strong>: 
Users should exercise caution when designing and/or deploying agentic AI applications in sensitive domains where agent actions are irreversible or highly consequential. Additional precautions should also be taken when creating autonomous agentic AI as described further in the <a href="/en/site-policy/github-terms">GitHub Terms</a>.</li>
<li><strong>Use Copilot code review to supplement human reviews, not to replace them</strong>: While Copilot code review can be a powerful tool for improving code quality, it is important to use it as a tool, rather than to replace human reviews. You should always review and verify the feedback generated by Copilot code review, and supplement Copilot&#x27;s feedback with careful human review to ensure your code meets your requirements.</li>
<li><strong>Provide feedback</strong>: If you encounter any issues or limitations with Copilot code review, we recommend that you provide feedback by using the thumbs up and thumbs down buttons on Copilot&#x27;s comments. This can help GitHub improve the tool and address any concerns or limitations.</li>
<li><strong>Configure custom instructions</strong>: You can configure custom instructions to help Copilot understand your coding style and best practices, improving the relevance and quality of review feedback.</li>
<li><strong>Ensure cloud agent tasks are well-scoped</strong>: The more clear and well-scoped the prompt you assign to the cloud agent, the better the results. An ideal issue includes a clear description of the problem, complete acceptance criteria, and hints on what files need to be changed.</li>
<li><strong>Customize the cloud agent with additional context</strong>: The cloud agent has access to semantic code search, which helps it find relevant code based on meaning rather than just exact text matches, allowing it to complete tasks faster. To further enhance performance, implement custom Copilot instructions to help the agent better understand your project and how to build, test, and validate its changes.</li>
<li><strong>Use the cloud agent as a tool, not a replacement</strong>: You should always review and test the content generated by the cloud agent to ensure that it meets your requirements and is free of errors or security concerns prior to merging.</li>
<li><strong>Use secure coding and code review practices with the cloud agent</strong>: Although the cloud agent can generate syntactically correct code, it may not always be secure. Follow best practices for secure coding and code review. Take the same precautions as you would with any code that uses material you did not independently originate, including rigorous testing, IP scanning, and checking for security vulnerabilities.</li>
<li><strong>Stay up to date</strong>: The cloud agent is an evolving technology. Stay up to date with any new security risks or best practices that may emerge.</li>
<li><strong>Use Copilot CLI as a tool, not a replacement</strong>: You should always review and verify commands and code generated by Copilot CLI to ensure they meet your requirements and are free of errors or security concerns.</li>
<li><strong>Review commands before execution (CLI)</strong>: Exercise particular caution when Copilot CLI suggests executing commands, especially those that modify or delete files. You are ultimately responsible for the commands you allow the agent to run.</li>
<li><strong>Keep CLI tasks well-scoped</strong>: The more clear and well-scoped the prompt you provide, the better the results. Include a clear description of the problem, acceptance criteria, and hints on what files need to be changed.</li>
<li><strong>Provide feedback (CLI)</strong>: If you encounter any issues or limitations with Copilot CLI, provide feedback using the <code>/feedback</code> command.</li>
<li><strong>Use direct tools when available (computer use)</strong>: When an API, MCP server, terminal command, filesystem tool, or dedicated browser tool can complete a task, use that tool for more structured information and predictable results.</li>
<li><strong>Keep computer-use tasks narrow and observable</strong>: Describe the outcome you want, the applications involved, and any important constraints. Review the active application and outcome.</li>
<li><strong>Limit always allowed applications</strong>: Avoid choosing <strong>Always allow</strong> for applications that contain sensitive information or support high-impact actions. Review and remove saved approvals when they are no longer needed.</li>
<li><strong>Validate custom agent behavior (SDK)</strong>: Thoroughly test custom agents, tools, and hooks before deploying applications built with the SDK to production. Ensure that tool configurations and system prompts produce safe, expected behavior.</li>
<li><strong>Audit MCP server connections (SDK)</strong>: Only connect to MCP servers that you trust. Review the tools and data that each server exposes and ensure they align with your application&#x27;s security requirements.</li>
<li><strong>Implement safety hooks (SDK)</strong>: Use the SDK&#x27;s lifecycle hooks to implement guardrails such as content filtering, audit logging, and tool approval workflows in your applications.</li>
<li><strong>Scope sessions appropriately (SDK)</strong>: Configure each SDK session with only the tools, agents, and permissions required for the task at hand. Avoid granting broad access when narrow scoping is sufficient.</li>
<li><strong>Review BYOK provider policies (SDK)</strong>: If using bring-your-own-key configurations, ensure your chosen model provider&#x27;s terms of service and data handling policies meet your organization&#x27;s requirements.</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="11-learn-more-about-github-copilot-agentic-features" tabindex="-1"><a class="heading-link" href="#11-learn-more-about-github-copilot-agentic-features">11. Learn more about GitHub Copilot agentic features<span class="heading-link-symbol" aria-hidden="true"></span></a></h2>
